该文章发表于2026年11月16日,没有明确提及事件日期,但提到了该药物于2026年10月获得美国监管机构批准以及中国正在进行的III期试验。
地点与对象
文章讨论了中国的情况,其中大约75万人患有慢性乙型肝炎,约有44万人被诊断,约有六百万人在接受治疗治疗。
事件
文章报道了葛兰素史克在将其乙肝药物贝匹罗韦森(Hibsago)推向中国市场时所面临的挑战。它强调了新 治疗 的潜力,但也强调了识别合适患者的困难以及与该药物相关的经济障碍。
规模、影响与风险
该药物在美国获得批准,部分患者可以停止用药,试验中 24% 的患者符合停止用药的标准。然而,文章指出,这些患者中只有 19% 在 24 周后达到“功能性治愈”。该药物还带有针对严重肝脏反应和血小板计数低的黑框警告。
应对、状态与不确定性
葛兰素史克针对中国的监管决定预计将于2027年上半年做出。该公司已与正大天晴合作进行分销和医院准入,但文章指出,由于资格标准和经济因素,该药物在中国的实际市场可能小于患者总数。证据并未提供明确的结果或解决方案。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

China Market for New Hepatitis B Drugs Is Large, Actual Sales Prospects Smaller - www.citynewsservice.cn

News Brief
The article, published on November 16, 2026, discusses the challenges of introducing GSK's hepatitis B drug, bepirovirsen (Hibsago), to the Chinese market. While China has a large population of people living with chronic hepatitis B—approximately 75 million—with around six million currently on treatment, the actual market for the drug may be smaller due to strict eligibility criteria. In the US, the drug was approved in October 2026, allowing some patients to stop medication if their viral levels are suppressed and their surface antigen levels are below 3,000 international units per milliliter. However, only 24% of patients in trials met these criteria, and 19% achieved a "functional cure" after 24 weeks. The drug carries a boxed warning for serious liver reactions and low platelet counts. GSK has partnered with Chia Tai Tianqing to distribute the drug in China, but the article notes that the economics of the drug, including high costs and reimbursement challenges, may limit its adoption. The regulatory decision for China is expected in the first half of 2027, and the article highlights the need for further research and cost considerations before the drug can be widely used.
Original source text (Korean)
한국어日本語Login 한국어日本語 Search 한국어日本語Login HOMESHANGHAI DAILYCHINA BIZ BUZZEVENTSARTICLESCOMMUNITY F&B City News Hai Lights Hai Guide Lifestyle SH Health Directory Shanghai City News Service Submit Event Home > Articles > China Market for New Hepatitis B Drugs Is Large, Actual Sales Prospects Smaller [Biopharma] China Market for New Hepatitis B Drugs Is Large, Actual Sales Prospects Smaller by Tan Weiyun November 16, 2026 [Biopharma] Share Article: Caption: GSK's bepirovirsen, sold under the brand name Hibsago, won US regulatory approval in October. Seventy-five million people make for a compelling market. Finding patients who will actually take a new medicine is a more demanding business. That is the challenge facing UK pharma giant GSK as it prepares to bring its hepatitis B drug to China – a development Chinese drugmakers are watching closely as they develop their own new treatments for the viral liver disease. GSK's bepirovirsen, sold under the brand name Hibsago, won US regulatory approval in October, offering some patients the possibility of stopping medication while keeping the infection under control. At the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases meeting in Denver earlier this month, Chinese drugmakers Hengrui Pharma and Brii Biosciences presented research pursuing the same broad ambition. For companies hoping to build a business around that promise, China's vast patient population is only the starting point. The nearer-term opportunity lies within a much smaller group already receiving care, and narrows further once the requirements of a new treatment are applied. GSK's own investor presentation illustrates the gap. Of an estimated 75 million people living with chronic hepatitis B in China, about 44 million have been diagnosed and around six million are on treatment. The company said in October that bepirovirsen remained under priority review in China, with a regulatory decision expected in the first half of 2027. Even those six million currently on treatment cannot simply be counted as prospective customers. Under its US approval, Hibsago is available to adults without cirrhosis who have received antiviral treatment for at least six months, have suppressed virus levels and have hepatitis B surface antigen levels of 3,000 international units per milliliter. The antigen is a viral protein measured in blood. China's eventual prescribing rules will determine eligibility there. The published figures do not establish how many Chinese patients would qualify. Nor do they tell companies how many eligible patients would choose treatment, obtain coverage or pay for it themselves. For a patient whose infection is already controlled with daily pills, the attraction is the chance to stop treatment. But that possibility comes with additional treatment and monitoring, without any guarantee of success. In the trials supporting US approval, 24 percent of patients receiving bepirovirsen met the criteria to stop their existing antiviral medication. Twenty-four weeks after stopping all hepatitis B treatment, 19 percent had maintained viral control with no detectable surface antigen – an outcome known as a "functional cure." Most patients did not reach that endpoint. The drug also carries a boxed warning for serious liver reactions and low platelet counts. Hou Jinlin, director of the Department of Infectious Diseases at Nanfang Hospital and a lead investigator in GSK's global phase III bepirovirsen trials, said the studies enrolled patients with surface antigen levels above 100 and up to 3,000 units. He explained that earlier research suggested patients with very low antigen levels could sometimes achieve clearance with existing treatment, while those with higher levels were harder to treat. He made the comments in an interview with the Yicai news group last week. "New drugs usually face high price barriers when they are first approved," Hou told Yicai, adding that reimbursement negotiations and lower costs as the industry matures could improve access. The economics may prove as demanding as the medicine. Credit: Imaginechina In Japan, Hibsago was listed in October at 183,015 yen (about US$1,150) per 150 milligram syringe. At the standard dosage, a 24-week course requires 52 syringes, putting the drug cost at roughly 9.5 million yen, before any insurance coverage. In the US, prescription discount platform GoodRx listed a coupon price of US$12,989 for five syringes as of November 12, although GSK has yet to disclose an official US list price. The two figures reflect different pricing systems and do not indicate what the drug might cost in China. With relatively inexpensive antivirals already keeping the virus under control for many patients, a new treatment will need to justify not only its price but also the cost of monitoring and the likelihood of a lasting response. GSK has already chosen a partner to help reach those patients in China. In May, it signed an exclusive agreement with Chia Tai Tianqing, a subsidiary of Sino Biopharmaceutical, whose commercial network covers more than 5,000 medical centers. The Chinese company will handle imports, distribution, hospital access and promotion in mainland China, while GSK retains regulatory, quality and drug-safety responsibilities. The deal puts an established hospital network behind a medicine that requires doctors to identify suitable patients and follow them through treatment. It also gives the Chinese company a stake in GSK's launch while others work on potential alternatives. Hengrui's HRS-5635 is among them. A phase II study of the experimental RNA-based medicine appeared in the same November 7 conference session as GSK's late-stage research. Hengrui's interim report lists the program as having entered phase III development in China. Brii, meanwhile, is testing whether combining an RNA-based drug with treatments that stimulate the immune system can help more patients respond. Its program includes BRII-179, a therapeutic vaccine intended for people already infected with hepatitis B. One finding from Brii's Denver presentation speaks directly to the challenge of expanding treatment options. In its phase II Enrich study, 11 of 53 participants with starting surface antigen levels above 1,000 and up to 3,000 units per milliliter maintained antigen loss 24 weeks after the study treatment ended. These patients are considered harder to treat, but their antigen levels still fall within the range covered by GSK's US approval. The small study group does not establish a broader eligible market or superiority over GSK. It does, however, offer a potential route to improving responses among patients less likely to benefit from existing approaches. Brii is also investigating whether it can shorten the interferon component of its combination treatment. If confirmed in further trials, a less burdensome course could make a difference to patients weighing a new treatment against the daily pills they already take. But Brii faces a hurdle beyond clinical performance. A dispute with US-based Vir Biotechnology over the transfer of manufacturing responsibilities for elebsiran, a key component of its combination therapy, has complicated its development plans. Brii said in August that it was not advancing an elebsiran-containing hepatitis B regimen into phase III while the arbitration remained unresolved. For later entrants, these are potential points of distinction. Better response rates, simpler treatment or fewer safety concerns could give doctors a reason to choose another medicine. But each advantage will need evidence from larger studies. Percentages drawn from different trials cannot settle the comparison. There is also a distinction between winning patients already in care and reaching those who have yet to start treatment. The former requires identifying suitable candidates within existing clinics. The latter entails more diagnosis, referral and sustained engagement with the health system. That makes the 75 million figure a measure of the disease's burden, rather than a ready-made sales forecast. GSK has secured a route into thousands of Chinese medical centers. Hengrui and Brii are still assembling the evidence for medicines that could follow. Their prospects will depend on the choices made inside those clinics: Who qualifies, who is willing to try and who can afford a chance to put the pill bottle away. 乙型肝炎检测的差距正在影响患者的旅程

Gaps in hepatitis B testing are shaping patient journeys
新闻简报The article highlights gaps in hepatitis B testing awareness and access in Canada, particularly in Manitoba. It notes that nearly nine in 10 Canadians have little to no awareness of chronic hepatitis B, which affects over 262,000 people. The Canadian Association for the Study of the Liver (CASL) recommends universal screening for all adult Canadians, regardless of birthplace. However, access to quantitative hepatitis B surface antigen (qHBsAg) testing, which is crucial for monitoring the virus's activity and guiding treatment, remains uneven across the country. This lack of access makes it difficult for patients to receive consistent care and informed decisions about their health. Dr. Scott Fung emphasizes the importance of screening and the role of healthcare providers in identifying and managing hepatitis B. The article calls for better integration of qHBsAg testing into provincial systems to improve outcomes and reduce hospitalizations for advanced liver disease.

时间
虽然大多数加拿大人都听说过乙型肝炎这个词,但事实是,这个国家的大多数人对这种潜在的严重肝脏疾病知之甚少。
地点与对象
加拿大,特别是曼尼托巴省,重点关注成年加拿大人。
事件
一项研究强调了乙型肝炎检测意识和获取方面的差距,特别是在马尼托巴省,并强调了加拿大肝脏研究协会 (CASL) 建议的 慢性乙型肝炎 (CHB) 普遍筛查的重要性。
规模、影响与风险
慢性乙型肝炎 影响加拿大超过 262,000 人,缺乏定量乙型肝炎表面抗原 (qHBsAg) 检测使长期管理变得复杂,并增加了肝硬化和肝癌等严重并发症的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
该文章讨论了改善 qHBsAg 检测和普遍筛查的必要性,但没有提供有关当前调查状态、检测实施情况或卫生当局采取的具体行动的信息。

Gaps in hepatitis B testing are shaping patient journeys

While most Canadians have heard of the term hepatitis B, the truth is that the majority of people in this country know very little about the potentially serious liver disease. Read unlimited articles for free today: or Already have an account? Log in here » While most Canadians have heard of the term hepatitis B, the truth is that the majority of people in this country know very little about the potentially serious liver disease. In fact, a study released earlier this year showed nearly nine in 10 Canadians have little to no awareness of chronic hepatitis B, despite the fact that the long-term viral infection affects more than 262,000 people in this country. So, what exactly is hepatitis B? In simple terms, it is a chronic viral infection of the liver that is spread through the bloodstream. It can lead to cirrhosis, in which healthy liver tissue is replaced by scar tissue, and can create a much higher risk of liver cancer and other complications such as ascites and jaundice. What makes hepatitis B particularly insidious is the fact that it is essentially a silent condition. In most cases, an individual afflicted with the disease does not display any symptoms, especially during the early or chronic stages of the infection. “It’s really a testament to how strong the liver is in most patients, even with a chronic viral infection. You have to have at least 90 per cent of the function of the liver affected before patients display any symptoms like swelling of the abdomen or jaundice or fatigue,” says Dr. Scott Fung, director of education for the Toronto Centre for Liver Disease and an Associate Professor of Medicine at the University of Toronto. “That’s why most patients with hepatitis B don’t complain of any major symptoms and they’re often quite surprised when they are diagnosed through blood testing that they have a chronic infection.” Dr. Fung says that’s why it’s important for adult Canadians to be screened for hepatitis B: they may already have it and not even know it. He’s not alone in that opinion. In 2025, the Canadian Association for the Study of the Liver (CASL) came up with new guidelines that endorse universal screening for all adult Canadians for hepatitis B at least once in their lifetime, regardless of where they were born. There are a number of different testing options available to Canadians. However, the recommended and most effective test is known as the hepatitis B triple-panel. Dr. Fung says triple-panel screening is particularly effective because even if a person tests negative for the surface antibody, it means there is still an opportunity for health prevention and promotion, including the injection of a safe and effective hepatitis B vaccination. Beyond initial screening for those with known hepatitis B infection, more advanced blood tests are increasingly important for long-term management. Quantitative hepatitis B surface antigen (qHBsAg) testing – a simple blood test that tracks how active the virus is in the liver over time – helps doctors see whether treatment is working and better gauge a patient’s risk of serious complications such as cirrhosis and liver cancer. However, the only catch is that access to quantitative hepatitis B surface antigen testing, recommended as standard of care by CASL, remains uneven and often difficult to navigate in many parts of the country, including here in Manitoba. Yet qHBsAg testing is not routinely available through many provincial laboratory systems, meaning that people who live with hepatitis B may need closer monitoring but still be unable to access the testing needed to inform those decisions. “For many of our patients, the real challenge is getting consistent screening and follow-up for chronic hepatitis B. If access to tests like quantitative HBsAg is limited, it becomes much harder to understand how active the virus is and to make informed decisions about long-term treatment,” says Dr. Fung. That’s why ensuring both basic screening and more advanced tests such as qHBsAg are available is essential, so people living with CHB can be diagnosed, monitored and supported consistently. For patients, these tests are straightforward blood draws that can be done during routine lab visits, but they play a critical role in deciding who needs closer follow-up and which treatments are most appropriate. From a public-health perspective, investing in quantitative HBsAg testing is a practical, affordable step. When this simple blood test is funded and integrated into provincial laboratory systems, it helps clinicians better monitor chronic hepatitis B over time, guide treatment decisions and reduce costly hospitalizations for advanced liver disease, while aligning provincial practice with national CHB guidelines. Dr. Fung also recommends that patients bring up the issue of hepatitis B screening with their personal healthcare provider. “The whole point behind universal screening is you don’t have to think about a patient’s risk factors, and the patient may not even remember what their risk factors are for hepatitis B,” adds Dr. Fung. The good news is that even if someone tests positive for chronic hepatitis B, the condition can be effectively managed. For people who need treatment, antiviral medications can suppress the virus and help reduce the risk of liver complications. Dr. Fung’s suggestion is that patients who receive a positive test result should work with their doctor to develop a plan on how to manage their condition. That should include follow-up testing to determine how their liver is performing in terms of producing proteins, metabolizing various toxins, the level of scarring or stiffness in the patient’s liver and what the risk is of developing complications such as liver cancer. Dr. Fung explains it’s important for people who have been impacted by hepatitis B to understand that, thanks to advances in research and antiviral treatments, the disease doesn’t have to have life-altering consequences. “We’ve been told many of these patients are suffering in silence. They have no voice and often isolate at home. They avoid certain social activities,” he says. “Once they’ve been diagnosed with hepatitis B, that should no longer be the case because we have effective treatments. It’s a highly treatable disease. We can help the patient lower their risk of complications and lower the risk of transmissibility to others. But we need to help overcome some of the social barriers that many of these patients face.” This article is produced by the Advertising Department of the Winnipeg Free Press, in collaboration with GSK

对于有限疗程治疗而言,停药后的持久性恰恰是衡量临床价值的重要维度。南方医科大学南方医院侯金林教授在接受《感染在线》采访时表示,AHB-137长期随访中达到完全应答并停用全部治疗的患者,持久应答率超过90%,这一结果为功能性治愈的长期维持提供了新的证据。
